03Verification, Validation, and Design ReviewTwelve checks passed, one evaluation still open+

Your goal

Report completed specification checks without implying that intended-use evaluation or release authorization is complete.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Twelve checks passed, one evaluation still open

Verification lead Arun and development manager Beth review the status of a fictional device. All twelve planned specification checks in the current package passed. The separate intended-user evaluation has not started. The slide nevertheless says validation complete and ready for release. No release authorization is supplied. A review meeting is scheduled tomorrow. Arun and Beth must present the completed evidence, the unperformed work, and the decision still required without treating the meeting itself as approval.

Arun: Verification lead / Beth: Development manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has passed?

Answer and explanation

C. Twelve planned specification checks in the current package

The briefing limits the passed work to twelve specification checks in one package.

2. What has not started?

Answer and explanation

B. The separate intended-user evaluation

The intended-user evaluation is explicitly unperformed in the supplied status.

3. What does tomorrow's meeting establish now?

Answer and explanation

B. Only that a review is scheduled

Scheduling a review does not supply its conclusions or an authorization in advance.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The twelve checks support the specified requirements they address, in the tested configuration. They do not automatically cover every user need, later version, or intended-use condition. Report completion at the level supported by the actual evidence.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
verification
Confirmation with evidence that specified requirements have been fulfilled.complete design verification
validation
Confirmation with evidence that requirements for an intended use are fulfilled.plan design validation
design review
A systematic evaluation of design progress, evidence, and issues.hold a design review
test protocol
A documented plan describing how a test will be performed and assessed.approve a test protocol
test report
A record of test execution, results, and conclusions.review a test report
test article
The item or configuration used in a test.identify the test article
configuration
The defined arrangement and versions of a product's elements.control the test configuration
requirement coverage
The extent to which requirements are addressed by specified evidence.assess requirement coverage
traceability matrix
A structured table linking requirements to related evidence.update the traceability matrix
test deviation
A departure from the planned test method or conditions.assess a test deviation
unresolved anomaly
An observed irregularity whose significance or resolution remains open.review unresolved anomalies
acceptance result
The recorded outcome against a specified acceptance criterion.document the acceptance result
representativeness
How well an item or setting reflects the intended evaluated population or use.justify representativeness
simulated use
Evaluation under conditions designed to represent actual use.define simulated-use conditions
review action
A task assigned as a result of a review.track review actions
action closure
Documented completion and acceptance of an assigned task.verify action closure
release authorization
A formal decision permitting a product or version to be released.obtain release authorization
design transfer
Translation of the design into production specifications and processes.review design transfer
document control
Management of document versions, approval, and availability.maintain document control
change impact
The consequences of a proposed or implemented alteration.assess change impact
regression testing
Rechecking affected behavior after a change.plan regression testing
evidence package
The organized records supporting a specific conclusion or decision.assemble the evidence package
review quorum
The required participation for a particular review under its procedure.confirm the review quorum
decision record
A documented conclusion, rationale, and associated conditions.maintain a decision record

A scheduled design review is not a completed decision. A review action is not closed merely because someone accepts ownership. Distinguish the test result, the remaining evaluation, the review conclusion, and the authorized release decision.

C Notice the language

Use complete with an object

A status update can be confident and narrow at the same time. Say which package is complete and which criterion it addresses. Replacing a global green status with separate evidence and decision lines helps colleagues see progress without confusing a finished test with a finished product.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the completed work
All twelve checks in this specification package passed.
Limit the conclusion
That result applies to the tested configuration and covered requirements.
Name the open evaluation
The intended-user evaluation has not started.
Correct the status
Replace validation complete with specification checks complete.
Request the link
Which requirement does this result address?
Ask about deviations
Were any departures from the protocol assessed and documented?
Separate the decision
A passed test package is not release authorization.
Describe the meeting
The review is scheduled; its conclusion is not yet available.
Keep actions visible
Assign an owner and closure evidence for each open item.
Check the version
Confirm that the report covers the version presented for review.
Request the rationale
What supports the representativeness of this test article?
Assess a change
A later modification needs an impact assessment before relying on these results.
Avoid a global claim
We have not demonstrated every intended-use requirement.
Clarify the handoff
The review package should show completed evidence and unresolved work separately.
Record the conclusion
Capture the actual decision and any conditions after the review.
Close accurately
Ready for review does not mean ready for release.
6 language and precision notes
Complete
State exactly which task, package, or decision is complete.
Passed
Applies to specified criteria under stated test conditions.
Representative
Needs a basis; visual similarity alone is not enough.
Pending
Identifies unresolved work without implying failure or success.
Authorized
Describes a decision by the responsible authority, not general enthusiasm.
Covered
Means linked evidence exists for the named requirement, not for every possible claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which slide heading is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Twelve specification checks passed; intended-user evaluation not started.

The heading retains the actual passed work and the explicitly unperformed evaluation.

2. A software revision follows the tests. What wording is best?

Answer and explanation

C. Assess the change before assuming the earlier results cover the new version.

The impact of the change determines what previous evidence remains applicable and what further work is needed.

3. A reviewer accepts ownership of an open test deviation. Which status can be recorded at that point?

Answer and explanation

B. Owner assigned; required assessment, completion evidence, and acceptance still outstanding

Assignment establishes who will do the work, not its technical conclusion, completion, acceptance, or release effect. Those require their own evidence and decisions.

4. Which conclusion may Beth report before the review?

Answer and explanation

D. The evidence package is ready for the scheduled review, with the remaining work identified.

The available facts support readiness for review but do not supply its outcome or release authorization.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Twelve checks passed, one evaluation still open

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Arun: Verification lead / Beth: Development manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • change impact
  • configuration
  • test deviation
  • Action closure
  • decision record
  • Release authorization
  • verification
  • requirement coverage
  • validation
  • evidence package
  1. Beth
    The dashboard says validation complete because all twelve checks passed. Before tomorrow's review, I want to be sure that headline means what the team thinks it means.
  2. Arun
    The completed work is 1 against the specifications in this package. The separate intended-user evaluation has not started, so the headline combines two different claims.
  3. Beth
    The test result is still good progress. We should not hide it, but we should identify what those twelve checks actually cover and what remains unperformed.
  4. Arun
    Exactly. We cannot mark 2 complete from these checks alone. The report should distinguish specification fulfillment from the evidence still needed about the intended use.
  5. Beth
    Can we say every requirement passed? That would be shorter, although I suspect the word every would extend the claim beyond this particular set of checks.
  6. Arun
    Show the 3 instead. Each result needs a clear link to its requirement, and any uncovered requirements must remain visible in the review package.
  7. Beth
    I also noticed that the report refers to the earlier software build. The team discussed a revision, but I do not know whether it has been implemented.
  8. Arun
    We must confirm the 4. Evidence belongs to the item actually tested; a discussion about a later build cannot silently extend the report to that version.
  9. Beth
    If the revision goes ahead, which test conclusions might it affect? Please flag that question now so the release review does not inherit mismatched versions.
  10. Arun
    That calls for a 5 assessment. We should not assume that every old result becomes invalid, or that every result automatically remains sufficient.
  11. Beth
    The dashboard also says ready for release. I intended ready for tomorrow's meeting, but I can see that those are not interchangeable status descriptions.
  12. Arun
    Correct. 6 is a separate decision. We have not been given that authorization, and scheduling a review does not establish its future outcome.
  13. Beth
    I will change the status to ready for review and put the unperformed intended-user evaluation beside the completed specification package. That should prevent a misleading green summary.
  14. Arun
    Include the 7, not just the totals. Reviewers need the reports, relevant versions, criteria, and any departures from the planned method.
  15. Beth
    There was one departure noted during execution. I do not want to describe it as a failed requirement if that is not what happened.
  16. Arun
    Call it a 8 and reference its assessment. The record should explain its significance and disposition without treating the label itself as a pass or failure.
  17. Beth
    Tomorrow, each unresolved item needs an owner. But the minutes should not show those items as closed simply because someone agrees to take responsibility.
  18. Arun
    Yes. 9 needs the required evidence and acceptance. Ownership makes follow-up possible; it does not demonstrate that the remaining task has already been completed.
  19. Beth
    I will separate the twelve passed checks from the unstarted user evaluation. Please confirm the tested build and keep release authorization off the slide until it exists.
  20. Arun
    After the meeting, add the actual 10 and its conditions. That keeps completed testing, open evaluation, review conclusions, and release status distinct throughout the handoff.

Answers and explanations
  1. verification Turn 2. The specification checks confirm specified requirements, which is the verification claim supported by the case.
  2. validation Turn 4. Validation concerns requirements for intended use, and the separate intended-user evaluation is still unperformed.
  3. requirement coverage Turn 6. Coverage identifies the requirements actually addressed by the available evidence rather than implying universal completeness.
  4. configuration Turn 8. The configuration identifies the versions and arrangement actually tested, limiting the scope of the result.
  5. change impact Turn 10. A change-impact assessment examines whether a modification affects the relevance of existing evidence or requires more work.
  6. Release authorization Turn 12. Release authorization is a distinct formal decision and is not supplied by a test result or meeting invitation.
  7. evidence package Turn 14. The evidence package organizes the actual records supporting the review rather than relying on a dashboard color.
  8. test deviation Turn 16. A deviation is a departure from the planned test, whose significance must be assessed rather than assumed.
  9. Action closure Turn 18. Closure requires the agreed completion evidence and acceptance, not merely assignment of responsibility.
  10. decision record Turn 20. The decision record captures the review's real conclusion and conditions after the review occurs.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Beth: The dashboard says validation complete because all twelve checks passed. Before tomorrow's review, I want to be sure that headline means what the team thinks it means.
  2. Arun: The completed work is verification against the specifications in this package. The separate intended-user evaluation has not started, so the headline combines two different claims.
  3. Beth: The test result is still good progress. We should not hide it, but we should identify what those twelve checks actually cover and what remains unperformed.
  4. Arun: Exactly. We cannot mark validation complete from these checks alone. The report should distinguish specification fulfillment from the evidence still needed about the intended use.
  5. Beth: Can we say every requirement passed? That would be shorter, although I suspect the word every would extend the claim beyond this particular set of checks.
  6. Arun: Show the requirement coverage instead. Each result needs a clear link to its requirement, and any uncovered requirements must remain visible in the review package.
  7. Beth: I also noticed that the report refers to the earlier software build. The team discussed a revision, but I do not know whether it has been implemented.
  8. Arun: We must confirm the configuration. Evidence belongs to the item actually tested; a discussion about a later build cannot silently extend the report to that version.
  9. Beth: If the revision goes ahead, which test conclusions might it affect? Please flag that question now so the release review does not inherit mismatched versions.
  10. Arun: That calls for a change impact assessment. We should not assume that every old result becomes invalid, or that every result automatically remains sufficient.
  11. Beth: The dashboard also says ready for release. I intended ready for tomorrow's meeting, but I can see that those are not interchangeable status descriptions.
  12. Arun: Correct. Release authorization is a separate decision. We have not been given that authorization, and scheduling a review does not establish its future outcome.
  13. Beth: I will change the status to ready for review and put the unperformed intended-user evaluation beside the completed specification package. That should prevent a misleading green summary.
  14. Arun: Include the evidence package, not just the totals. Reviewers need the reports, relevant versions, criteria, and any departures from the planned method.
  15. Beth: There was one departure noted during execution. I do not want to describe it as a failed requirement if that is not what happened.
  16. Arun: Call it a test deviation and reference its assessment. The record should explain its significance and disposition without treating the label itself as a pass or failure.
  17. Beth: Tomorrow, each unresolved item needs an owner. But the minutes should not show those items as closed simply because someone agrees to take responsibility.
  18. Arun: Yes. Action closure needs the required evidence and acceptance. Ownership makes follow-up possible; it does not demonstrate that the remaining task has already been completed.
  19. Beth: I will separate the twelve passed checks from the unstarted user evaluation. Please confirm the tested build and keep release authorization off the slide until it exists.
  20. Arun: After the meeting, add the actual decision record and its conditions. That keeps completed testing, open evaluation, review conclusions, and release status distinct throughout the handoff.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Specifications checked, user needs still under evaluation

Fictional design review: outputs have been checked against specifications; the planned evaluation of intended user needs is incomplete.

  1. Design manager: We've checked the outputs against specifications. Can we cancel the remaining user-needs evaluation to save time?
  2. Validation lead: Those activities address different questions. Verification checks specifications; validation addresses intended needs in the relevant use context.
  3. Design manager: How should we compare the value of the completed tests and the planned evaluation?
  4. Validation lead: Against their respective objectives and acceptance criteria, not by treating either as a substitute for the other.
  5. Design manager: The remaining evaluation takes longer. Could we call the faster route equally complete?
  6. Validation lead: No. The time saving would leave an identified evidence gap rather than establish equivalent coverage.
  7. Design manager: I'll present completed verification separately and keep the validation activity in the review plan.
  8. Validation lead: Please specify which user-needs questions remain open so the schedule discussion has a concrete basis.
  9. Design manager: I'll ask the design-review team to assess the options without declaring the device fully validated.
  10. Validation lead: Good. We can compare timing and evidence coverage while preserving the different purposes of the work.
Bench timing versus task completion with users

Separate fictional planning case: a bench test measures button response time; a proposed user study measures whether technicians complete the intended setup task.

  1. Test engineer: The bench test is quick. Could we use it instead of the technician setup study?
  2. Human factors lead: It measures button response time, while the study addresses completion of the intended user task.
  3. Test engineer: Both involve the same button. Why aren't their acceptance criteria interchangeable?
  4. Human factors lead: A responsive button doesn't establish that users understand when and how to use it in the task.
  5. Test engineer: How can we compare the two activities fairly when management asks which one to fund?
  6. Human factors lead: Show the question each addresses, evidence coverage, effort, and unresolved gaps if an activity is deferred.
  7. Test engineer: Could we report bench verification complete while leaving the user-task evidence pending?
  8. Human factors lead: Yes, if those statuses match the records. Don't call the pending evidence a passing validation result.
  9. Test engineer: I'll bring that comparison to design review and avoid ranking the options by duration alone.
  10. Human factors lead: I'll identify the intended-user questions so the decision maker sees the actual tradeoff.
A changed criterion breaks a simple pass-rate comparison

Separate fictional verification review: revision A used one acceptance criterion, while revision B uses a different criterion; both have reported pass percentages.

  1. Quality manager: Revision B has a higher pass percentage. Can we say its design is better verified?
  2. Verification engineer: The acceptance criteria changed, so the percentages don't provide a like-for-like comparison.
  3. Quality manager: Could we ignore the change because both reports use the word pass?
  4. Verification engineer: No. Pass means meeting the criterion used in that test, and those criteria differ.
  5. Quality manager: What comparison would let the design-review team understand actual progress and remaining coverage?
  6. Verification engineer: Map the requirements and criteria across revisions, then identify comparable tests and changed evidence needs.
  7. Quality manager: Would rerunning everything be the only possible response to the difference?
  8. Verification engineer: We haven't assessed that. The authorized review should determine the needed work from the changes.
  9. Quality manager: I'll qualify the percentages and ask for a requirement-level comparison before presenting an improvement claim.
  10. Verification engineer: I'll show the differences and uncertainties without treating a higher headline rate as proof of broader validation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Twelve checks passed, one evaluation still open
  1. Read the review exchange. State twelve passed specification checks and the intended-user evaluation not started.
  2. Swap roles. Distinguish the tested configuration, tomorrow's review, and the release decision still unmade.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A review date is not a decision

Six specification checks passed on version B. The intended-use evaluation is pending, and a review is booked for Friday. No release decision has been made.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "Six checks passed on version ___."

Answer and explanation

A. B

The evidence is explicitly tied to the supplied tested version.

2. Engineer: "The intended-use evaluation remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

The briefing gives no completed evaluation or successful result.

3. Lead: "Friday is the scheduled ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

A booked meeting identifies a review date rather than a release decision.

4. Engineer: "Release has not been ___."

Answer and explanation

D. authorized

No release decision has been made in the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

Lesson 03: Verification, Validation, and Design Review
Original fictional case: Verification lead Arun and development manager Beth review the status of a fictional device. All twelve planned specification checks in the current package passed. The separate intended-user evaluation has not started. The slide nevertheless says validation complete and ready for release. No release authorization is supplied. A review meeting is scheduled tomorrow. Arun and Beth must present the completed evidence, the unperformed work, and the decision still required without treating the meeting itself as approval.
Language workshop: Twelve checks passed, one evaluation still open
Communication goal: Report completed specification checks without implying that intended-use evaluation or release authorization is complete.
Language guidance: The twelve checks support the specified requirements they address, in the tested configuration. They do not automatically cover every user need, later version, or intended-use condition. Report completion at the level supported by the actual evidence.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): All twelve checks in this specification package passed. / That result applies to the tested configuration and covered requirements. / The intended-user evaluation has not started. / Replace validation complete with specification checks complete. / Which requirement does this result address? / Were any departures from the protocol assessed and documented? / A passed test package is not release authorization. / The review is scheduled; its conclusion is not yet available. / Assign an owner and closure evidence for each open item. / Confirm that the report covers the version presented for review. / What supports the representativeness of this test article? / A later modification needs an impact assessment before relying on these results. / We have not demonstrated every intended-use requirement. / The review package should show completed evidence and unresolved work separately. / Capture the actual decision and any conditions after the review. / Ready for review does not mean ready for release.
Vocabulary: verification: Confirmation with evidence that specified requirements have been fulfilled. / validation: Confirmation with evidence that requirements for an intended use are fulfilled. / design review: A systematic evaluation of design progress, evidence, and issues. / test protocol: A documented plan describing how a test will be performed and assessed. / test report: A record of test execution, results, and conclusions. / test article: The item or configuration used in a test. / configuration: The defined arrangement and versions of a product's elements. / requirement coverage: The extent to which requirements are addressed by specified evidence. / traceability matrix: A structured table linking requirements to related evidence. / test deviation: A departure from the planned test method or conditions. / unresolved anomaly: An observed irregularity whose significance or resolution remains open. / acceptance result: The recorded outcome against a specified acceptance criterion. / representativeness: How well an item or setting reflects the intended evaluated population or use. / simulated use: Evaluation under conditions designed to represent actual use. / review action: A task assigned as a result of a review. / action closure: Documented completion and acceptance of an assigned task. / release authorization: A formal decision permitting a product or version to be released. / design transfer: Translation of the design into production specifications and processes. / document control: Management of document versions, approval, and availability. / change impact: The consequences of a proposed or implemented alteration. / regression testing: Rechecking affected behavior after a change. / evidence package: The organized records supporting a specific conclusion or decision. / review quorum: The required participation for a particular review under its procedure. / decision record: A documented conclusion, rationale, and associated conditions.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Usability and Human FactorsThree participants read the label differently+

Your goal

Report observed use difficulties neutrally and distinguish exploratory findings from validation or population-level rates.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Three participants read the label differently

Human-factors specialist Laila and interface designer Victor review a formative study of a fictional device label. Three of eight participants selected the wrong status label during a simulated task. The draft summary calls them careless and concludes that a larger font will solve the problem. No revised label has been tested, and the study does not establish a population error rate. Laila and Victor must preserve the observation, investigate the interface, and describe the proposed revision without declaring success.

Laila: Human-factors specialist / Victor: Interface designer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was observed?

Answer and explanation

D. Three of eight participants selected the wrong status label in the simulated task.

The observation is limited to the stated participants, label, and simulated task.

2. Which wording adds a cause not established by the three wrong selections?

Answer and explanation

D. Participants selected incorrectly because they were careless.

The study records a behavior, not carelessness as its cause. Wrong selection does not by itself establish misreading, misunderstanding, inattention, or any single explanation.

3. What is the status of the larger font?

Answer and explanation

A. An untested proposed revision

The revised label has not been tested, so its effect is not established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three of eight is 37.5% of this study group, not an established error rate for the user population. Report the count and study context. A formative finding can identify a design concern without providing a final validation conclusion.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
human factors
Study and application of how people interact with systems and products.apply human-factors principles
usability engineering
A structured approach to evaluating and improving user interaction.conduct usability engineering
formative evaluation
An exploratory assessment used to inform design development.run a formative evaluation
summative evaluation
An assessment of a sufficiently developed design against defined evaluation goals.plan a summative evaluation
human-factors validation
Evaluation of intended users performing relevant tasks under representative conditions.plan human-factors validation
critical task
A task whose error or omission could seriously harm a patient or user, including compromised care.identify critical tasks
use error
An action or omission producing a result different from that intended or expected.analyze a use error
close call
A situation in which a problem nearly occurs but is avoided.document a close call
use difficulty
Observed trouble completing or understanding a task.record a use difficulty
participant
A person taking part in a study.recruit representative participants
moderator
The person guiding a study session under its protocol.brief the moderator
prompting
Assistance that may influence a participant's performance.document moderator prompting
task success
Completion of a defined task under stated evaluation criteria.define task success
observation note
A factual record of behavior or events during a study.write an observation note
root-cause analysis
Investigation of underlying contributors to an observed problem.conduct root-cause analysis
mental model
A user's understanding of how a system works.explore the user's mental model
affordance
A feature suggesting how an object or control can be used.assess interface affordances
label comprehension
Understanding the meaning conveyed by a label.evaluate label comprehension
visual hierarchy
The arrangement that makes some information more prominent than other information.improve the visual hierarchy
legibility
How readily individual text or symbols can be distinguished.assess label legibility
cognitive load
The mental effort needed to process information and perform a task.reduce cognitive load
training decay
Loss of retained training knowledge or skill over time.consider training decay
representative sample
A participant group suited to the intended study population and question.justify the representative sample
design iteration
A revised version created through successive development cycles.evaluate a design iteration

Legibility asks whether text can be distinguished; comprehension asks whether its meaning is understood. Increasing font size may affect one without resolving the other. A proposed revision needs evaluation rather than a sentence declaring it successful.

C Notice the language

Describe the interaction before judging it

Careless assigns a personal explanation that an observation may not support. Describe what participants saw, did, and said, then examine the task and interface. Neutral language can be more demanding than blame because it requires the team to specify evidence for its explanation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Report the observation
Three of eight participants selected the wrong status label.
Preserve the context
This occurred in the simulated task used in the formative study.
Remove blame
The notes do not establish that the participants were careless.
Separate behavior from cause
The selection is observed; the reason still needs investigation.
Ask about wording
Did participants distinguish the meanings of the two labels?
Ask about visibility
Was the relevant text readily legible under the study conditions?
Check assistance
Record whether the moderator prompted the participant.
Bound the percentage
That percentage describes this study group, not the user population.
Mark the revision
A larger font is proposed but has not been evaluated.
Avoid a success claim
We cannot yet say the change resolves the problem.
Compare explanations
Font size, wording, and hierarchy are different possible contributors.
Keep evidence intact
Retain the observation notes when revising the summary.
Clarify task risk
Assess the task's safety significance through the appropriate process.
Name the evaluation stage
These formative findings inform development; they are not a completed validation claim.
Request a scoped follow-up
Evaluate the revised label against the specific difficulty observed.
Close the report
State the finding, the open explanation, and the next evaluation separately.
6 language and precision notes
Observed
Identifies what was recorded rather than why it happened.
Careless
Assigns blame that the evidence may not support.
Formative
Describes work intended to inform design development.
Resolves
Claims an outcome that needs evidence from the revised design.
Legible
Readable at the text or symbol level, not necessarily understood.
Representative
Depends on users, tasks, and conditions, not only participant count.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Three of eight participants selected the wrong label in the formative simulated task.

The accurate summary preserves the observed count and the study's scope without adding a cause or outcome.

2. A participant can read both labels aloud but confuses their meanings. Which issue is most directly suggested?

Answer and explanation

B. Label comprehension

Reading the characters while confusing their meaning points to comprehension rather than legibility alone.

3. How should assistance during a task be reported?

Answer and explanation

B. Document the prompting rather than presenting assisted success as unassisted performance.

Prompting can affect the interpretation of performance and must remain visible in the record.

4. Which next-status sentence fits the facts?

Answer and explanation

D. The larger-font revision is proposed; its effect on the observed difficulty remains untested.

The supplied facts contain a proposal but no evaluation of that revised label.

Laila: Human-factors specialist / Victor: Interface designer

    Then keep the 1 factual: which label, which task, and what happened. Remove the character judgment without losing the actual difficulty.

    Would thirty-seven point five percent sound more precise in the headline? I worry the count looks small, even though the issue may matter.

    The team wants a larger font. That is easy to implement, but I cannot yet say that small characters caused all three wrong selections.

    3 is one question. We also need to examine whether the words distinguish the two states clearly in the task context.

    One person read both labels aloud accurately and still selected the wrong status. Larger letters would not necessarily address that person's interpretation.

    Another person finished after the moderator asked a follow-up question. The results table currently says success, with no note about the intervention.
  10. Laila
    Record the 5. We need to see how completion was achieved; assisted success should not silently become unassisted task performance.

    What about placing the status higher on the screen? That changes where attention goes, rather than only changing the text size.
  12. Laila
    It is a 6 proposal. We can evaluate it alongside wording, but neither idea is a demonstrated solution yet.
  13. Victor
    I will stop calling the larger font a fix. The revised label has not been tested, and we may need a different approach.
  14. Laila
    Call it a proposed 7. Link it to the observed difficulty and keep the follow-up result open rather than writing success in advance.
  15. Victor
    The project lead also asks whether a wrong selection makes this a critical task. Three errors alone do not answer the safety question, do they?
  16. Laila
    No. Designating a 8 depends on potential consequences, including compromised care. The relevant risk assessment must address that, not just the number of selections.
  17. Victor
    Then I should not describe this as our final usability validation either. We used the session to find weaknesses while the interface was still developing.
  18. Laila
    Correct. Completed 9 is not established by a formative session and an untested revision. State the evaluation stage and conditions clearly.
  19. Victor
    I will retain the three-of-eight count, the reading-versus-meaning observation, and the assisted completion. Proposed changes will sit in a separate section.
  20. Laila
    That gives the 10 useful evidence. We can investigate the difficulty without blaming participants or promising that one easy design change resolves it.

Answers and explanations
  1. observation note Turn 2. An observation note records what participants did without supplying an unsupported explanation for their behavior.
  2. formative evaluation Turn 4. A formative evaluation informs design development and does not establish a population error rate or completed validation.
  3. Legibility Turn 6. Legibility concerns distinguishing text or symbols, which font size may affect without resolving meaning.
  4. label comprehension Turn 8. Reading text correctly while misunderstanding its meaning suggests a comprehension issue rather than only a visibility problem.
  5. prompting Turn 10. Moderator prompting can influence performance and must remain visible when interpreting task completion.
  6. visual hierarchy Turn 12. Visual hierarchy concerns the relative prominence and arrangement of information, beyond the size of individual characters.
  7. design iteration Turn 14. A design iteration is a revised version to evaluate, not evidence that the underlying problem is resolved.
  8. critical task Turn 16. Critical-task status depends on the potential harm of incorrect or omitted performance and requires the appropriate assessment.
  9. human-factors validation Turn 18. The supplied exploratory study and untested revision do not establish completed human-factors validation.
  10. root-cause analysis Turn 20. Root-cause analysis examines contributors to the observed difficulty rather than accepting an unsupported blame statement.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Misread labels without calling participants careless

Fictional formative usability review: several participants misread a label, but the report supplies no evidence about motivation or attentiveness.

  1. Usability researcher: The draft says participants were careless. Our observation was that several misread the label.
  2. Design lead: Could carelessness be a reasonable explanation for the use error in this formative study?
  3. Usability researcher: It is an untested interpretation, not an observed fact. We should investigate the interaction and context.
  4. Design lead: What description would help the team act without assigning blame to the participants?
  5. Usability researcher: State which label was misread, during which task, and what the participants actually did.
  6. Design lead: Those detailed observations need checking against the study records before we add them.
  7. Usability researcher: Exactly. Don't invent the sequence or a cause just to complete the paragraph.
  8. Design lead: Could we already describe this as a failed summative study because use errors occurred?
  9. Usability researcher: No. This was formative work, and its purpose and evidence must be described accurately.
  10. Design lead: I'll remove the blame language and request the observed task details for a focused human-factors review.
Four pauses are an observation, not a diagnosis of confusion

Separate fictional formative study: four of eight technicians paused before selecting a menu item; interviews about their reasons are not yet reviewed.

  1. Product manager: Four of eight technicians paused. Can we say half the users were confused by the menu?
  2. Human factors researcher: We observed pauses, but haven't reviewed their explanations. Confusion is not yet established.
  3. Product manager: Would the timing alone show that the interface caused the hesitation during the task?
  4. Human factors researcher: It identifies behavior to investigate, not the cause by itself.
  5. Product manager: How should the finding describe the sample without generalizing to every intended user?
  6. Human factors researcher: Say four of eight participants in this formative study paused before selection, with reasons still under review.
  7. Product manager: Could we pair that observation with the interview evidence once you've checked it?
  8. Human factors researcher: Yes. Keep participant reports distinct from observed actions and our interpretation.
  9. Product manager: I'll remove the half of all users headline and preserve the actual study count.
  10. Human factors researcher: I'll review the accounts and identify which design questions are supported, without assuming a particular use-error mechanism.
Moderator help limits an unaided-success claim

Separate fictional usability session: one participant completed a setup task after the moderator gave a hint; unaided completion was not observed for that attempt.

  1. Study coordinator: The participant finished the task. Can I count this attempt as unaided success?
  2. Usability specialist: No. The moderator gave a hint, so the completion occurred with assistance.
  3. Study coordinator: The hint was short. Could we leave it out because the participant performed the final action?
  4. Usability specialist: That would remove context relevant to interpretation. Record both the assistance and the observed completion.
  5. Study coordinator: Does the assisted attempt prove that every user would fail without help?
  6. Usability specialist: No. That broader conclusion isn't supported by this single formative observation.
  7. Study coordinator: I'll label the attempt assisted and preserve the moderator's wording in the study record.
  8. Usability specialist: Good. Any summary should follow the study's definitions rather than silently change the success category.
  9. Study coordinator: Should we discuss the unanswered unaided-task question at the next human-factors review?
  10. Usability specialist: Yes. Identify the evidence gap and appropriate follow-up without relabeling this attempt as a summative validation result.

Three participants read the label differently
  1. Read the formative-study discussion. State three of eight wrong selections and distinguish behavior from an inferred cause.
  2. Swap roles. Contrast readable text with understood meaning and assisted completion with unassisted performance.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Readable is not necessarily understood

Two of ten participants read a label accurately but misunderstand its meaning in a formative session. Revised wording is proposed and has not been tested.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Researcher: "Two of ten had difficulty with the label's ___."

Answer and explanation

C. meaning

The participants could read the text but misunderstood what it conveyed.

2. Designer: "This is an observation from a formative ___."

Answer and explanation

C. session

The supplied result comes from a developmental evaluation, not a population study.

3. Researcher: "The new wording remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. untested

No evaluation of the proposed wording has been performed.

4. Designer: "We cannot yet claim the difficulty is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. resolved

A proposed revision does not demonstrate that the observed problem has been corrected.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Medical Devices English

Scope: Original fictional language practice, not device operating instructions or medical, legal, quality-system, or regulatory advice. Lesson 04: Usability and Human Factors
Original fictional case: Human-factors specialist Laila and interface designer Victor review a formative study of a fictional device label. Three of eight participants selected the wrong status label during a simulated task. The draft summary calls them careless and concludes that a larger font will solve the problem. No revised label has been tested, and the study does not establish a population error rate. Laila and Victor must preserve the observation, investigate the interface, and describe the proposed revision without declaring success.
Language workshop: Three participants read the label differently
Communication goal: Report observed use difficulties neutrally and distinguish exploratory findings from validation or population-level rates.
Language guidance: Three of eight is 37.5% of this study group, not an established error rate for the user population. Report the count and study context. A formative finding can identify a design concern without providing a final validation conclusion.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Three of eight participants selected the wrong status label. / This occurred in the simulated task used in the formative study. / The notes do not establish that the participants were careless. / The selection is observed; the reason still needs investigation. / Did participants distinguish the meanings of the two labels? / Was the relevant text readily legible under the study conditions? / Record whether the moderator prompted the participant. / That percentage describes this study group, not the user population. / A larger font is proposed but has not been evaluated. / We cannot yet say the change resolves the problem. / Font size, wording, and hierarchy are different possible contributors. / Retain the observation notes when revising the summary. / Assess the task's safety significance through the appropriate process. / These formative findings inform development; they are not a completed validation claim. / Evaluate the revised label against the specific difficulty observed. / State the finding, the open explanation, and the next evaluation separately.
Vocabulary: human factors: Study and application of how people interact with systems and products. / usability engineering: A structured approach to evaluating and improving user interaction. / formative evaluation: An exploratory assessment used to inform design development. / summative evaluation: An assessment of a sufficiently developed design against defined evaluation goals. / human-factors validation: Evaluation of intended users performing relevant tasks under representative conditions. / critical task: A task whose error or omission could seriously harm a patient or user, including compromised care. / use error: An action or omission producing a result different from that intended or expected. / close call: A situation in which a problem nearly occurs but is avoided. / use difficulty: Observed trouble completing or understanding a task. / participant: A person taking part in a study. / moderator: The person guiding a study session under its protocol. / prompting: Assistance that may influence a participant's performance. / task success: Completion of a defined task under stated evaluation criteria. / observation note: A factual record of behavior or events during a study. / root-cause analysis: Investigation of underlying contributors to an observed problem. / mental model: A user's understanding of how a system works. / affordance: A feature suggesting how an object or control can be used. / label comprehension: Understanding the meaning conveyed by a label. / visual hierarchy: The arrangement that makes some information more prominent than other information. / legibility: How readily individual text or symbols can be distinguished. / cognitive load: The mental effort needed to process information and perform a task. / training decay: Loss of retained training knowledge or skill over time. / representative sample: A participant group suited to the intended study population and question. / design iteration: A revised version created through successive development cycles.
Speaking task: Read the formative-study discussion. State three of eight wrong selections and distinguish behavior from an inferred cause. Swap roles. Contrast readable text with understood meaning and assisted completion with unassisted performance. Read the corrected two-of-ten transfer. Keep the revised wording untested and the comprehension issue unresolved.
Partner: Laila: Human-factors specialist / Victor: Interface designer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two of ten participants read a label accurately but misunderstand its meaning in a formative session. Revised wording is proposed and has not been tested.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Victor: Then I should not describe this as our final usability validation either. We used the session to find weaknesses while the interface was still developing.
Laila: Correct. Completed human-factors validation is not established by a formative session and an untested revision. State the evaluation stage and conditions clearly.
Victor: I will retain the three-of-eight count, the reading-versus-meaning observation, and the assisted completion. Proposed changes will sit in a separate section.
Laila: That gives the root-cause analysis useful evidence. We can investigate the difficulty without blaming participants or promising that one easy design change resolves it.
END REFERENCE
05Regulatory Pathways and SubmissionsA route under review is not a clearance+

Your goal

Distinguish a proposed US submission pathway, supporting evidence, and an actual regulatory decision.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A route under review is not a clearance

Regulatory specialist Inez and commercial director Caleb review a fictional US launch plan. The team is evaluating a possible 510(k) pathway and has identified a candidate predicate, but its suitability is still under review. No submission has been filed and no FDA decision exists. A sales slide says FDA approved and promises an October launch. Inez must correct both the status and the certainty of the timeline while preserving a clear planning discussion.

Inez: Regulatory specialist / Caleb: Commercial director

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the current pathway status?

Answer and explanation

B. A possible 510(k) route is being evaluated.

The briefing identifies an ongoing pathway assessment, not a completed submission or decision.

2. What is known about the predicate?

Answer and explanation

D. A candidate has been identified, but suitability remains under review.

The candidate's identification does not settle whether it supports the proposed pathway.

3. Why is the October launch promise unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. No filing or regulatory decision exists, and the pathway assessment is incomplete.

The case supplies unresolved dependencies rather than a guaranteed launch authorization or date.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

For a US 510(k), clearance is the relevant decision term; it should not be casually called PMA approval. A candidate predicate, a pathway assessment, a submitted file, and an agency decision are four different statuses.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
regulatory pathway
The applicable route for obtaining a required regulatory decision.assess the regulatory pathway
device classification
A regulatory category reflecting the device type and applicable controls.confirm device classification
product code
A code used by FDA to identify a device type.verify the product code
510(k)
A US premarket notification seeking a substantial-equivalence determination.prepare a 510(k)
predicate device
A legally marketed comparator used in a 510(k) assessment.assess a candidate predicate
substantial equivalence
The comparative regulatory determination central to a 510(k) decision.support substantial equivalence
De Novo request
A US request for risk-based classification of a novel eligible device.assess a De Novo request
premarket approval
The US PMA pathway requiring an independent safety-and-effectiveness demonstration.prepare a premarket approval application
clearance
A favorable 510(k) determination permitting marketing within its scope.obtain 510(k) clearance
approval
A formal favorable decision under an applicable approval pathway.distinguish approval from clearance
indications for use
The specific purposes and population described for a device's use.define indications for use
technological characteristics
Design and operating features relevant to a regulatory comparison.compare technological characteristics
general controls
Baseline regulatory controls applicable to medical devices.comply with general controls
special controls
Additional controls applicable to certain device types.identify special controls
submission dossier
The organized documents and evidence submitted for regulatory review.assemble a submission dossier
evidence plan
A plan for generating and organizing support for specified claims.develop an evidence plan
regulatory strategy
A coordinated approach to regulatory requirements and decisions.align the regulatory strategy
pre-submission interaction
A planned exchange with FDA before a later submission.request a pre-submission interaction
review question
A request for clarification or evidence during review.address a review question
deficiency
A shortcoming identified in the submitted information.respond to a deficiency
response package
Documents addressing specified reviewer questions or deficiencies.compile a response package
marketing authorization
The applicable permission to market a device within a defined scope.confirm marketing authorization
regulatory dependency
An unresolved regulatory matter affecting another planned step.identify a regulatory dependency
launch assumption
A planning premise underlying the proposed market-entry date.qualify a launch assumption

A proposed pathway does not determine the outcome or guarantee a date. The actual device, claims, classification, and evidence matter. These fictional facts do not establish eligibility for 510(k), De Novo, PMA, or an exemption.

C Notice the language

Correct status without inventing the opposite

An unsupported approval claim should be replaced with the actual status, not with a prediction that the device will fail. Separate the pathway assessment, evidence plan, submission, review, and decision. This gives commercial colleagues information they can use without a guarantee the team cannot make.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the current stage
We are evaluating a possible 510(k) pathway.
Qualify the comparator
The predicate candidate remains under review.
Correct the decision claim
No FDA decision has been issued for this device.
Name the missing filing
The submission has not yet been filed.
Use the right term
A favorable 510(k) determination is clearance, not PMA approval.
Separate similarity from evidence
Similar appearance alone does not establish substantial equivalence.
Clarify the claim
The proposed indications must be consistent across the evidence and materials.
Bound the timeline
October is a planning assumption, not an authorized launch date.
Identify dependencies
The pathway assessment and evidence requirements remain unresolved.
Avoid predicting rejection
An open assessment does not mean the device has been rejected.
Request specialist review
Regulatory needs to assess the proposed use and comparison.
Describe an interaction
A pre-submission discussion is not a marketing authorization.
Align the slide
Replace FDA approved with the actual development status.
Keep decisions traceable
Use the exact decision and scope when an authorization exists.
Name the next deliverable
The next step is the pathway assessment, not a launch announcement.
Close the planning discussion
We can plan around dependencies without promising the agency's decision.
6 language and precision notes
Candidate
Identifies an option being assessed rather than an accepted comparator.
Filed
Reports submission, not a favorable review outcome.
Cleared
Use for an actual favorable 510(k) determination within its scope.
Approved
Do not use as a generic synonym for every regulatory interaction.
Expected
A forecast needs assumptions and should not sound like a guarantee.
Under review
Specify whether the review is internal or by the agency.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sales-slide correction matches the case?

Answer and explanation

B. Possible 510(k) pathway under internal assessment; no submission or FDA decision yet.

The correction describes the actual internal stage without inventing approval, timing, or rejection.

2. Which statement about the candidate predicate is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Identification begins the assessment; suitability and the comparative evidence still need review.

A named candidate is not itself an accepted comparison or a regulatory decision.

3. Which October-launch note preserves the current regulatory status?

Answer and explanation

C. Internal planning target; pathway and predicate still under assessment, with no filing or decision

The date is internal and conditional. Neither internal approval, a candidate comparator, nor a pathway discussion starts or settles the required agency decision.

4. What does a pre-submission interaction establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

C. An opportunity for feedback, not marketing authorization

A pre-submission exchange does not itself supply the later marketing decision.

A route under review is not a clearance

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Inez: Regulatory specialist / Caleb: Commercial director

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  2. Inez
    Neither is an authorization. We are still assessing the 1, including whether a 510(k) route is appropriate for the proposed device and its claims.
  3. Caleb
    The team found a marketed device that looks similar. I took that as confirmation that the route was settled, but the comparison may not be that simple.
  4. Inez
    It is a candidate 2. Its suitability remains under review; visual similarity alone does not establish the regulatory comparison needed for the proposed use.
  5. Caleb
    What should the commercial team understand about that comparison? I want the next discussion to focus on the evidence rather than repeat the same assumption.
  6. Inez
    The relevant determination is 3. The proposed use and technological characteristics matter, and the evidence must support the actual comparison rather than a convenient headline.
  7. Caleb
    Nothing has been filed, correct? Then under review is too vague; sales could read that as an FDA review already underway.
  8. Inez
    Exactly. The 4 is not filed. Say internal pathway assessment, and keep that separate from any future agency review or decision.
  9. Caleb
    If the team eventually follows the 510(k) route successfully, should the slide say approved? That term is common in sales conversations, but I want it to be accurate.
  10. Inez
    Use 5 for the actual favorable 510(k) determination. Do not borrow the language of a different pathway or imply a decision before one exists.
  11. Caleb
    Could a different route still be needed? I do not want the revised slide to make the 510(k) plan sound irrevocable while the assessment is incomplete.
  12. Inez

  13. Caleb
    The product team also wants broader wording about who can use the device. That seems connected to the pathway work, not just to marketing style.
  14. Inez
    It is. The 7 must be clear and consistent. A broader statement can change the claim being assessed and the evidence needed to support it.
  15. Caleb
    Then October should be shown as an internal target with conditions. We cannot treat it as an agency commitment when neither the pathway nor the evidence package is settled.
  16. Inez
    Call it a 8 and show the dependencies. That allows planning without making a promise about a regulatory decision that has not occurred.
  17. Caleb
    We may seek feedback before filing. I will make sure the team does not describe a discussion with the agency as permission to market the device.
  18. Inez
    Correct. A 9 is not the marketing decision. Any feedback and its scope should be recorded accurately without converting it into blanket approval.
  19. Caleb
    I will remove FDA approved and mark October as conditional. Send me the pathway-assessment milestone and its dependencies for the planning slide.
  20. Inez
    Good. The communication must distinguish planning from 10. We can describe the work confidently while leaving the unresolved regulatory decision exactly where it belongs.

Answers and explanations
  1. regulatory pathway Turn 2. The pathway is the route being evaluated, not an agency decision already obtained.
  2. predicate device Turn 4. A candidate predicate is a comparator under assessment, not proof that the route or outcome is settled.
  3. substantial equivalence Turn 6. Substantial equivalence is the comparative regulatory determination sought through a 510(k), not a claim established by appearance.
  4. submission dossier Turn 8. The dossier is the organized submission material, which has not yet been filed in this case.
  5. clearance Turn 10. Clearance is the appropriate favorable 510(k) decision term, distinct from PMA approval.
  6. regulatory strategy Turn 12. The strategy coordinates the applicable route and evidence; it remains subject to the unresolved assessment.
  7. indications for use Turn 14. Indications describe the specific proposed purposes and population, which must align with the regulatory assessment and evidence.
  8. launch assumption Turn 16. The October date is a conditional planning premise rather than an authorized or guaranteed launch date.
  9. pre-submission interaction Turn 18. A pre-submission interaction can provide feedback but does not itself authorize marketing.
  10. marketing authorization Turn 20. Marketing authorization is the applicable permission to market within a defined scope, which this case does not supply.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Request a pathway assessment, not a presumed route

Fictional submission planning: a slide presents the proposed device pathway as settled, although regulatory specialists are still evaluating options.

  1. Project manager: The slide presents our proposed pathway as settled. What confirmation should I request before using it?
  2. Regulatory lead: Ask for the current assessment, supporting assumptions, and unresolved questions from the regulatory team.
  3. Project manager: Could I leave 510(k) as confirmed because it is already printed in the timeline?
  4. Regulatory lead: No. A planning label doesn't establish that route or an agency outcome.
  5. Project manager: What specific action do you need from the product team for the assessment?
  6. Regulatory lead: Please confirm the current device description and intended use before our internal review meeting.
  7. Project manager: Should the slide list De Novo and PMA as equally appropriate alternatives while review continues?
  8. Regulatory lead: Not unless assessed. We can say pathway under evaluation without inventing a ranking or conclusion.
  9. Project manager: I'll correct the status and send the requested product information for review.
  10. Regulatory lead: I'll return a scoped assessment and identify which questions need further specialist or agency clarification.
A similar product is not a confirmed predicate

Separate fictional regulatory exercise: a competitor's brochure describes a superficially similar device; no predicate assessment has been completed.

  1. Commercial manager: This brochure looks similar to our device. Can we identify it as our confirmed predicate?
  2. Regulatory specialist: Not from the brochure alone. We need the relevant device information and a regulatory assessment.
  3. Commercial manager: What can I request from our research team before tomorrow's strategy discussion?
  4. Regulatory specialist: Ask for the source documents supporting the comparison, including the device identity and intended-use information.
  5. Commercial manager: Would sharing the same marketing category prove suitability for a 510(k) strategy?
  6. Regulatory specialist: No. A marketing category is not a completed predicate-device analysis.
  7. Commercial manager: I'll call it a candidate for assessment rather than a selected predicate in the briefing.
  8. Regulatory specialist: Please also identify gaps instead of filling missing technical characteristics from appearance or promotional language.
  9. Commercial manager: I'll send the documented comparison questions today and request an initial review status.
  10. Regulatory specialist: I'll assess what is available and explain the next evidence needs without promising a pathway conclusion.
A design change needs a scoped regulatory question

Separate fictional planning call: a team proposes changing the device's intended user group; regulatory implications have not been reviewed.

  1. Development director: Can the regulatory team confirm that this user-group change has no submission implications?
  2. Regulatory strategist: Please frame it as an assessment request, not a request to endorse an assumed answer.
  3. Development director: What information would make the request focused enough for the team to act?
  4. Regulatory strategist: Provide the current and proposed user groups, intended tasks, and the related design-change description.
  5. Development director: Could we assume the original pathway remains unchanged because the hardware isn't changing?
  6. Regulatory strategist: We shouldn't infer that. The intended-use context needs review even if the hardware description stays the same.
  7. Development director: I'll request the assessment before we finalize the project plan, with the changes clearly compared.
  8. Regulatory strategist: Good. Ask for an initial scope and timing estimate rather than an immediate blanket assurance.
  9. Development director: I'll leave the pathway implications open and avoid making a 510(k), De Novo, or PMA claim.
  10. Regulatory strategist: I'll coordinate the relevant review and return the verified planning implications when the assessment is available.
