Vocabulary: regulatory pathway: The applicable route for obtaining a required regulatory decision. / device classification: A regulatory category reflecting the device type and applicable controls. / product code: A code used by FDA to identify a device type. / 510(k): A US premarket notification seeking a substantial-equivalence determination. / predicate device: A legally marketed comparator used in a 510(k) assessment. / substantial equivalence: The comparative regulatory determination central to a 510(k) decision. / De Novo request: A US request for risk-based classification of a novel eligible device. / premarket approval: The US PMA pathway requiring an independent safety-and-effectiveness demonstration. / clearance: A favorable 510(k) determination permitting marketing within its scope. / approval: A formal favorable decision under an applicable approval pathway. / indications for use: The specific purposes and population described for a device's use. / technological characteristics: Design and operating features relevant to a regulatory comparison. / general controls: Baseline regulatory controls applicable to medical devices. / special controls: Additional controls applicable to certain device types. / submission dossier: The organized documents and evidence submitted for regulatory review. / evidence plan: A plan for generating and organizing support for specified claims. / regulatory strategy: A coordinated approach to regulatory requirements and decisions. / pre-submission interaction: A planned exchange with FDA before a later submission. / review question: A request for clarification or evidence during review. / deficiency: A shortcoming identified in the submitted information. / response package: Documents addressing specified reviewer questions or deficiencies. / marketing authorization: The applicable permission to market a device within a defined scope. / regulatory dependency: An unresolved regulatory matter affecting another planned step. / launch assumption: A planning premise underlying the proposed market-entry date.
Speaking task: Read the launch discussion. Distinguish pathway assessment, candidate predicate, filing, and an actual agency decision. Swap roles. Use clearance for the 510(k) outcome and describe October as an internal conditional target. Read the corrected filing transfer. A filed submission with ongoing review is not a granted clearance.
Partner: Inez: Regulatory specialist / Caleb: Commercial director
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional US 510(k) submission has been filed. Review is ongoing, and no decision has been issued. The commercial team has an internal target date.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Caleb: We may seek feedback before filing. I will make sure the team does not describe a discussion with the agency as permission to market the device.
Inez: Correct. A pre-submission interaction is not the marketing decision. Any feedback and its scope should be recorded accurately without converting it into blanket approval.
Caleb: I will remove FDA approved and mark October as conditional. Send me the pathway-assessment milestone and its dependencies for the planning slide.
Inez: Good. The communication must distinguish planning from marketing authorization. We can describe the work confidently while leaving the unresolved regulatory decision exactly where it belongs.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 05: Regulatory Pathways and Submissions
Original fictional case: Regulatory specialist Inez and commercial director Caleb review a fictional US launch plan. The team is evaluating a possible 510(k) pathway and has identified a candidate predicate, but its suitability is still under review. No submission has been filed and no FDA decision exists. A sales slide says FDA approved and promises an October launch. Inez must correct both the status and the certainty of the timeline while preserving a clear planning discussion.
Language workshop: A route under review is not a clearance
Communication goal: Distinguish a proposed US submission pathway, supporting evidence, and an actual regulatory decision.
Language guidance: For a US 510(k), clearance is the relevant decision term; it should not be casually called PMA approval. A candidate predicate, a pathway assessment, a submitted file, and an agency decision are four different statuses.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We are evaluating a possible 510(k) pathway. / The predicate candidate remains under review. / No FDA decision has been issued for this device. / The submission has not yet been filed. / A favorable 510(k) determination is clearance, not PMA approval. / Similar appearance alone does not establish substantial equivalence. / The proposed indications must be consistent across the evidence and materials. / October is a planning assumption, not an authorized launch date. / The pathway assessment and evidence requirements remain unresolved. / An open assessment does not mean the device has been rejected. / Regulatory needs to assess the proposed use and comparison. / A pre-submission discussion is not a marketing authorization. / Replace FDA approved with the actual development status. / Use the exact decision and scope when an authorization exists. / The next step is the pathway assessment, not a launch announcement. / We can plan around dependencies without promising the agency's decision.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Complaints, MDRs, and Postmarket SignalsIncomplete information still needs a handoff+

Your goal

Capture a complaint accurately, route missing information promptly, and distinguish counts from rates and reports from causation.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Incomplete information still needs a handoff

Complaint specialist Nora and service lead Dev review a caller's report that a fictional monitor unexpectedly stopped displaying. The event date, device identifier, and patient outcome are not yet known. The call was received today at 14:10. Dev proposes waiting for every field before routing it. A separate dashboard shows eight complaints this quarter versus four last quarter, without use-volume data. Nora must preserve the unknowns, arrange the required internal review, and prevent either dataset from implying an unsupported conclusion.

Nora: Complaint specialist / Dev: Service lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which time is known?

Answer and explanation

B. The call-receipt time, 14:10 today

The supplied time records receipt of the call, not the underlying event.

2. What is known about patient outcome?

Answer and explanation

D. It is not yet known.

The case supplies no outcome, so neither absence nor presence of injury is established.

3. What does eight versus four establish?

Answer and explanation

C. The complaint count doubled, but no rate can be calculated from the supplied data.

Counts can be compared directly, but a use-based rate requires a relevant denominator and context.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The receipt time is not the event time. Unknown patient outcome is not the same as no injury. Preserve the reporter's account and route the available information through the applicable process while actively seeking missing facts.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
complaint
A reported concern about a device's quality, performance, safety, or related service.capture a complaint
adverse event
An unfavorable occurrence that may require assessment of its relationship to a device.assess an adverse event
malfunction
A failure to perform as intended or meet relevant performance specifications.investigate a reported malfunction
Medical Device Reporting
The US system for reporting specified device events under applicable requirements.assess Medical Device Reporting obligations
reportability
Whether an event meets applicable reporting requirements.assess reportability
initial reporter
The person or organization first providing event information.contact the initial reporter
awareness date
The date relevant event information becomes known for the applicable assessment.document the awareness date
event date
The date on which the reported occurrence took place.confirm the event date
receipt date
The date the organization received the communication.record the receipt date
Unique Device Identifier
A device-identification code with defined identification components.capture the Unique Device Identifier
serial number
An identifier distinguishing an individual unit.verify the serial number
lot number
An identifier linking items to a production batch.record the lot number
patient outcome
The reported effect on the patient's condition or health.clarify the patient outcome
causal relationship
A supported connection between a device and an observed event.assess the causal relationship
event narrative
A factual account of the reported occurrence and its context.preserve the event narrative
reasonably known information
Information available or obtainable as defined by the applicable reporting requirement.provide reasonably known information
follow-up attempt
A documented effort to obtain missing or clarifying information.record a follow-up attempt
supplemental report
A later report providing required additional information.submit a supplemental report
postmarket surveillance
Systematic collection and review of information after market introduction.conduct postmarket surveillance
safety signal
Information suggesting a potential safety issue that needs assessment.investigate a safety signal
trend analysis
Review of patterns in data over time.perform trend analysis
exposure denominator
The relevant use or exposure quantity against which events are compared.identify the exposure denominator
duplicate report
Another record potentially describing an already reported event.assess duplicate reports
complaint closure
Documented completion of the required complaint-review process.authorize complaint closure

Eight complaints versus four is a doubled count, not automatically a doubled rate per use or proof of causation. Reporting patterns, duplicates, exposure, and case differences matter. In this chapter, MDR means US Medical Device Reporting, not the EU Medical Device Regulation.

C Notice the language

Unknown is a useful status

A missing field can be stated clearly without sounding evasive. Say what was reported, what is unknown, who is seeking it, and where the information has been routed. This supports timely review while avoiding a false conclusion that no injury or device problem occurred.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Capture the account
The caller reported that the display stopped unexpectedly.
Separate dates
The call was received today; the event date is still unknown.
Mark a missing identifier
We have not yet confirmed the device identifier.
Preserve outcome uncertainty
Patient outcome has not been established.
Avoid causal certainty
This report does not by itself establish the cause.
Route available facts
Send the available information for prompt internal assessment.
Continue follow-up
Record the questions asked and the attempts to obtain missing information.
Distinguish the review
Reportability needs assessment under the applicable requirements.
Avoid a blanket rule
Missing fields do not justify withholding the initial internal handoff.
Use the right denominator
We need the relevant exposure data before describing a rate.
State the count
The count increased from four to eight.
Check duplicates
Confirm whether separate records describe the same event.
Avoid dismissal
An incomplete account still needs appropriate review.
Preserve the original
Keep the initial narrative alongside later clarifications.
Name the update route
New required information must follow the applicable reporting process.
Close with status
The case is routed, follow-up is open, and no cause is confirmed.
6 language and precision notes
Reported
Attributes information without treating every detail as independently verified.
Unknown
A distinct status, not a synonym for no or none.
Received
Describes the communication date, not necessarily when the event occurred.
Reportable
Requires the relevant criteria; not every complaint has the same reporting obligation.
Doubled
State whether a count, rate, or other measure doubled.
Caused
A causal conclusion needs more support than temporal association alone.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which intake sentence preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Display interruption reported; event date, identifier, and patient outcome unknown; call received today at 14:10.

The accurate sentence distinguishes supplied facts, unknown fields, and receipt time without inventing an outcome.

2. What is the appropriate internal handoff wording?

Answer and explanation

C. Route the available account promptly for assessment and continue documented follow-up.

Missing information should trigger follow-up rather than replace the required internal review.

3. What does the dashboard support?

Answer and explanation

D. The count rose from four to eight; the rate per use is not established.

The dashboard supplies counts, not exposure, unique-event confirmation, outcomes, or causation.

4. The caller later reports no injury. Which update preserves the information history and review responsibilities?

Answer and explanation

D. Add the attributed clarification and its receipt time; continue the applicable reporting assessment.

A later no-injury report resolves an outcome detail, not the initial knowledge state or every reporting criterion. Some malfunction circumstances can require reporting assessment even without an injury.

  12. Nora

  13. Dev

  14. Nora
    Then the 7 remains unconfirmed. Report the observation accurately without either asserting device causation or dismissing the device as unrelated before the evidence supports that conclusion.
  15. Dev

  16. Nora

  17. Dev

  19. Dev

    Good. Any required later information must follow the applicable 10 process. Keep investigation, reporting assessment, and complaint closure distinct so the record never suggests an unfinished task has already been completed.

Answers and explanations
  1. event narrative Turn 2. The event narrative preserves the reported occurrence while missing facts are pursued through follow-up.
  2. receipt date Turn 4. The supplied date and time concern receipt of the communication, not the unconfirmed event date.
  3. patient outcome Turn 6. Patient outcome is unknown here; lack of supplied information does not establish that no injury occurred.
  4. Unique Device Identifier Turn 8. The Unique Device Identifier helps identify the device, while unavailable identification details remain follow-up items.
  5. reportability Turn 10. Reportability depends on applicable criteria and the event assessment, not the intake team's unsupported assumption.
  6. follow-up attempt Turn 12. A follow-up attempt records efforts to obtain the information needed for the investigation and reporting assessment.
  7. causal relationship Turn 14. A causal relationship is not established merely because the caller reported an event involving the device.
  8. exposure denominator Turn 16. A rate relative to use requires the relevant exposure quantity, which the dashboard does not provide.
  9. duplicate report Turn 18. A duplicate report may describe an already recorded event, so record counts are not automatically unique-event counts.
  10. supplemental report Turn 20. A supplemental report provides required later information through the appropriate reporting process rather than silently altering history.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Nora: We should route the available event narrative for prompt internal assessment and continue follow-up. An incomplete account is not a reason to withhold it from the responsible team.
  3. Dev: I have routed the account now. The call arrived today at fourteen ten; the event date is still unknown.

  5. Dev: The caller did not tell us whether anyone was injured. The draft summary says no injury, which is more definite than the information actually supports.
  6. Nora: Change patient outcome to unknown. We should ask for clarification and record the response without treating silence in the first call as a confirmed absence of harm.
  7. Dev: We also need to identify the unit accurately. A model name alone might not let the investigation team distinguish this device from other units at the site.
  8. Nora: Seek the Unique Device Identifier and other relevant identification details through the approved process. Record what is available rather than inventing a serial number to complete the form.
  9. Dev: Is this automatically reportable because the display stopped? Service needs to avoid delay without deciding beyond its role.

  11. Dev: I will document the missing questions and the calls we make to obtain answers. The case should show what we tried, not simply remain blank.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A malfunction complaint with missing identifiers

Fictional complaint handoff: a malfunction is reported, but the device identifier and event date are missing; no reportability assessment exists.

  1. Customer support specialist: I'm handing over a reported malfunction; the device identifier and event date are still missing.
  2. Complaint investigator: Please pass the available account now through our complaint channel and label those gaps explicitly.
  3. Customer support specialist: Should I wait until the reporter provides both details before you receive the complaint?
  4. Complaint investigator: Don't delay this handoff for completeness. Follow our process for transferring known facts and requesting follow-up.
  5. Customer support specialist: Can I label it a confirmed MDR event to make the urgency clear?
  6. Complaint investigator: No reportability assessment is established here. Keep the complaint description separate from that authorized determination.
  7. Customer support specialist: I'll preserve the reporter's wording and avoid inventing the model or event date.
  8. Complaint investigator: I'll take ownership of investigation intake and coordinate the missing-information request.
  9. Customer support specialist: Please confirm that your team received the handoff so I don't assume responsibility transferred.
  10. Complaint investigator: Confirmed. We'll assess the available facts and keep the unresolved identifier, date, and reporting questions visible.
A second report may describe the same event

Separate fictional postmarket case: distributor and clinic reports mention the same device reference and date; whether they describe one event or two is unconfirmed.

  1. Postmarket analyst: We received two reports with the same device reference and date. I'm flagging a possible duplicate.
  2. Complaint coordinator: Have you confirmed that they describe one event rather than separate malfunctions?
  3. Postmarket analyst: No. The matching details suggest a check, but they don't establish duplication by themselves.
  4. Complaint coordinator: Please link both reports for review without deleting either account from the record.
  5. Postmarket analyst: Should the surveillance count show two confirmed events while the check is pending?
  6. Complaint coordinator: Distinguish reports received from verified event count so the uncertainty isn't hidden.
  7. Postmarket analyst: I'll hand over both source references and identify which details match.
  8. Complaint coordinator: I'll own the reconciliation and route any reporting assessment through the responsible team.
  9. Postmarket analyst: Can you return the verified relationship before I finalize the postmarket surveillance summary?
  10. Complaint coordinator: Yes. I'll report the reconciliation status without assuming either duplication or two independent events in advance.
A returned device does not complete the investigation

Separate fictional complaint case: the returned device has arrived at the laboratory, but examination and causality review have not started.

  1. Service coordinator: The returned device arrived this morning. I'm transferring the case to the investigation laboratory.
  2. Investigation engineer: Thank you. Receipt is confirmed, but examination and any causal assessment are still pending.
  3. Service coordinator: Could customer support say the reported malfunction has been verified now that we have the device?
  4. Investigation engineer: No. Possession of the device isn't confirmation of the reported behavior or its cause.
  5. Service coordinator: I'll pass the complaint reference and maintain the distinction between receipt and examination.
  6. Investigation engineer: Please include the available custody information through the case system so the handoff is traceable.
  7. Service coordinator: Who will return an update for the customer's next inquiry about investigation progress?
  8. Investigation engineer: I'll own the laboratory status update, while the complaint team retains its overall coordination role.
  9. Service coordinator: I'll tell support the device is received and assessment remains pending, without predicting findings.
  10. Investigation engineer: Correct. I'll confirm the next verified status and keep any MDR assessment with the authorized reporting team.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Incomplete information still needs a handoff
  1. Read the intake exchange. Route the account and confirm receipt before discussing missing details; preserve 14:10 as call receipt.
  2. Swap roles. Say outcome unknown rather than no injury, and doubled count rather than doubled use-based rate.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The report date is known

A caller reports a device issue today. The event date and outcome are unknown. Six complaints were recorded this month, but the number of uses is unavailable.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Specialist: "Today is the date the report was ___."

Answer and explanation

A. received

The case gives the communication date but not the event date.

2. Colleague: "The patient outcome is still ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unknown

No outcome was supplied, so absence of injury cannot be assumed.

3. Specialist: "Six is the complaint ___."

Answer and explanation

C. count

Six describes recorded complaints rather than a rate per use.

4. Colleague: "A use-based rate needs the exposure ___."

Answer and explanation

D. denominator

The relevant number of uses is missing from the supplied data.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

observed event. / event narrative: A factual account of the reported occurrence and its context. / reasonably known information: Information available or obtainable as defined by the applicable reporting requirement. / follow-up attempt: A documented effort to obtain missing or clarifying information. / supplemental report: A later report providing required additional information. / postmarket surveillance: Systematic collection and review of information after market introduction. / safety signal: Information suggesting a potential safety issue that needs assessment. / trend analysis: Review of patterns in data over time. / exposure denominator: The relevant use or exposure quantity against which events are compared. / duplicate report: Another record potentially describing an already reported event. / complaint closure: Documented completion of the required complaint-review process.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Manufacturing, Suppliers, and NonconformanceThree hundred pieces on hold+

Your goal

Separate affected-lot scope, observed failures, containment, cause, and authorized disposition in a supplier discussion.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Three hundred pieces on hold

Quality engineer Tomas and supply planner Grace review two incoming component lots containing 120 and 180 pieces. One sampled piece from each lot failed a specified dimensional check. Both lots are on hold under the fictional site procedure. The cause is not established, and no release or rework disposition is authorized. Production needs parts tomorrow. Grace proposes describing all 300 pieces as defective, then releasing the second lot because its supplier certificate says conforming.

Tomas: Quality engineer / Grace: Supply planner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many pieces are in the two held lots?

Answer and explanation

D. 300

The held-lot quantities are 120 and 180, which total 300 pieces.

2. How many sampled pieces are explicitly reported as failing?

Answer and explanation

C. Two, one from each lot

The brief reports two observed sampled failures, not inspection results for every piece.

3. What is the current disposition?

Answer and explanation

D. Both lots remain on hold; no release or rework is authorized.

The case supplies a hold and explicitly states that disposition has not been authorized.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The held population is 300 pieces. The supplied observation is two failed sampled pieces, one from each lot. Do not call all 300 individually confirmed defective, or treat uninspected pieces as conforming. The hold and the observed failures describe different scopes.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
incoming inspection
Examination of received material against specified requirements.perform incoming inspection
nonconformance
Failure to fulfill a specified requirement.document a nonconformance
nonconforming material
Material identified as not meeting a specified requirement.control nonconforming material
containment
Immediate action to limit the scope or spread of a problem.implement containment
quarantine
Controlled segregation or status preventing unintended use.place material in quarantine
disposition
An authorized decision on how identified material will be handled.approve material disposition
rework
Action intended to bring nonconforming product into conformity.authorize a rework instruction
scrap
Material designated not to be used for its intended production purpose.document a scrap decision
concession
An authorized permission to use or release specified nonconforming product under defined conditions.evaluate a concession request
certificate of conformity
A supplier statement that supplied material meets specified requirements.review the certificate of conformity
supplier corrective action request
A formal request for a supplier to address a quality problem.issue a supplier corrective action request
corrective action
Action addressing the cause of a nonconformity to prevent recurrence.verify corrective action
correction
Action addressing a detected nonconformity itself.distinguish correction from corrective action
CAPA
Corrective and preventive action within a quality-system process.assess the need for CAPA
lot genealogy
Records linking material lots through production and distribution.trace lot genealogy
acceptance sampling
Evaluation of a lot using a defined sample and decision plan.apply an acceptance-sampling plan
dimensional tolerance
The permitted variation in a specified measurement.check dimensional tolerance
measurement uncertainty
The quantified uncertainty associated with a measurement result.evaluate measurement uncertainty
calibration status
Whether a measuring instrument's calibration requirements are satisfied.verify calibration status
supplier qualification
Evaluation and approval of a supplier for a defined purpose.maintain supplier qualification
change notification
Communication of a proposed or implemented change affecting supply.require a change notification
release status
The current authorization state for using or distributing material.verify release status
Quality Management System Regulation
The current US device quality-system regulation, commonly called QMSR.follow the Quality Management System Regulation
ISO 13485
An international standard for medical-device quality management systems.apply ISO 13485 requirements

A supplier certificate does not cancel contrary inspection evidence or authorize release. Containment, correction, corrective action, and disposition are different steps. Since February 2026, the US framework is QMSR, incorporating ISO 13485:2016, rather than the former QSR framework.

C Notice the language

Be firm about status and exact about scope

Quality communication should not soften a hold into an informal preference, but it should not overstate the evidence either. Name the held population, the inspected failures, and the authorized next step separately. Production urgency can be acknowledged without becoming an unsupported release rationale.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the held population
Both lots are on hold, totaling 300 pieces.
State the observed failures
One sampled piece from each lot failed the dimensional check.
Avoid overstating scope
We have not inspected and confirmed every piece as defective.
Avoid assuming conformity
The uninspected pieces are not thereby proven conforming.
Name the requirement
Reference the specified dimension and applicable tolerance.
Preserve the hold
No release or rework disposition is authorized.
Acknowledge urgency
I understand tomorrow's need; the material status still has to be respected.
Assess contrary evidence
The certificate does not erase the failed inspection result.
Separate containment
The hold limits unintended use while the issue is assessed.
Keep cause open
The supplier cause has not been established.
Request a response
Ask for evidence addressing the specified nonconformance.
Clarify corrective action
Fixing a piece and preventing recurrence are different objectives.
Verify the instrument
Include calibration status and relevant measurement information in the review.
Trace the scope
Use lot records to assess what other material may be affected.
Define authorization
Only the designated process can approve the material disposition.
Close the handoff
Give production the confirmed status and the next review time, not an assumed release.
6 language and precision notes
Affected
Define whether this means held, inspected, failed, or potentially involved.
Defective
Avoid applying the label to an unexamined population without support.
Held
Describes a controlled status, not a final scrap decision.
Certificate
A supplier statement that remains subject to relevant acceptance evidence.
Rework
Needs the required authorization and controlled instructions.
Cause
An investigation conclusion, not something established by urgency or suspicion.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which quantity statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Three hundred pieces are held; two sampled pieces are explicitly reported as failing.

The statement distinguishes total held quantity from the two observed sampled failures.

2. Production proposes using the 180-piece lot because its certificate is conforming. Which response preserves the current evidence and authority?

Answer and explanation

B. The failed sample and hold remain; the certificate does not replace an authorized disposition.

The hold covers the whole lot. A certificate, missing individual results, and deadline do not supply the missing handling decision or cancel contrary inspection evidence.

3. Which statement distinguishes correction from corrective action?

Answer and explanation

C. Correction addresses the detected problem; corrective action addresses its cause to prevent recurrence.

The distinction concerns fixing the detected nonconformity versus addressing the cause of recurrence.

4. Which supplier update is best supported?

Answer and explanation

A. The cause remains open; provide relevant evidence while both lots retain their current hold status.

The supplied evidence supports an open investigation and continued stated hold, not a cause or release decision.

E Conversations

Three hundred pieces on hold

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tomas: Quality engineer / Grace: Supply planner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • release status
  • lot genealogy
  • correction
  • nonconformance
  • calibration status
  • containment
  • certificate of conformity
  • Incoming inspection
  • corrective action
  • disposition
  1. Grace
    Production needs the parts tomorrow. The lots contain 120 and 180 pieces. I was about to report all 300 as defective, but that overstates our inspection.
  2. Tomas
    1 found two failed sampled pieces, one per lot. Both lots are held; those are different quantities and different statements.
  3. Grace
    So the report needs three hundred held, two observed failures. We cannot assign an individual result to pieces that were not inspected.
  4. Tomas
    Correct. Link each 2 to the dimensional requirement and lot. Uninspected does not mean conforming, either; keep the hold status visible.
  5. Grace
    The 180-piece lot has a supplier certificate saying conforming. Can that support using it while we investigate the failed sample?
  6. Tomas
    The 3 does not override contrary inspection evidence. That lot remains held unless the authorized process establishes a different disposition.
  7. Grace
    I will tell production both lots remain unavailable. We have not established whether the two sampled failures have the same cause.
  8. Tomas
    Exactly. 4 can be in place while cause remains open. Controlling the material does not require us to pretend the investigation is complete.
  9. Grace
    Could I ask quality about rework tonight? I need to offer production a next step without accidentally authorizing work myself.
  10. Tomas
    Request a 5 review. Do not tell anyone to rework or use material before the required decision, instructions, and evaluation are in place.
  11. Grace
    Then tonight is a request for review, not an expected release. I should give production the actual status and the next update we can commit to.
  12. Tomas
    Keep 6 separate from the deadline. Schedule pressure can make the review urgent; it cannot provide evidence or approval.
  13. Grace
    The supplier offers to replace the two failed samples. That might address those particular pieces, but does not explain the dimensional problem.
  14. Tomas
    Replacement could be a 7 through the approved process. It would not by itself establish the cause or prevent the same issue recurring.
  15. Grace
    I will request their manufacturing and inspection records. We should also examine our measurement setup rather than begin by assigning fault to the supplier.
  16. Tomas
    Include our instrument's 8 and the relevant results. A certificate or a suspicion is not a substitute for the evidence from either side.
  17. Grace
    Once a cause is supported, I will ask what action addresses it and how effectiveness will be checked. A promise about future lots is not enough.
  18. Tomas
    That is the purpose of 9. Distinguish the action against recurrence from replacing a piece or maintaining the current hold.
  19. Grace
    For now: three hundred held, two sampled failures, cause open, no release or rework authorized. I will send that exact status to production.
  20. Tomas
    Use 10 to check whether other material may be involved. Keep any wider scope qualified until the records support it.

Answers and explanations
  1. Incoming inspection Turn 2. Incoming inspection produced two sampled failures; it did not establish individual results for every piece.
  2. nonconformance Turn 4. The nonconformance is the failure against the specified dimensional requirement, with its observed scope documented.
  3. certificate of conformity Turn 6. A certificate is a supplier statement and does not override contrary inspection evidence or the current hold.
  4. containment Turn 8. Containment limits unintended use while the scope and cause are investigated; it is not a final causal conclusion.
  5. disposition Turn 10. Disposition is the authorized handling decision, which has not yet been made for these lots.
  6. release status Turn 12. Release status records whether use is authorized, independently of schedule pressure or a hoped-for resolution.
  7. correction Turn 14. Correction addresses a detected nonconformity itself and does not necessarily resolve the underlying cause.
  8. calibration status Turn 16. Calibration status is relevant measurement information to review, not automatic proof that either party caused the problem.
  9. corrective action Turn 18. Corrective action addresses a cause to prevent recurrence, with evidence needed to support its effectiveness.
  10. lot genealogy Turn 20. Lot genealogy links material through production and distribution, supporting assessment of any wider affected scope.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Grace: Production needs the parts tomorrow. The lots contain 120 and 180 pieces. I was about to report all 300 as defective, but that overstates our inspection.
  2. Tomas: Incoming inspection found two failed sampled pieces, one per lot. Both lots are held; those are different quantities and different statements.
  3. Grace: So the report needs three hundred held, two observed failures. We cannot assign an individual result to pieces that were not inspected.
  4. Tomas: Correct. Link each nonconformance to the dimensional requirement and lot. Uninspected does not mean conforming, either; keep the hold status visible.
  5. Grace: The 180-piece lot has a supplier certificate saying conforming. Can that support using it while we investigate the failed sample?
  6. Tomas: The certificate of conformity does not override contrary inspection evidence. That lot remains held unless the authorized process establishes a different disposition.
  7. Grace: I will tell production both lots remain unavailable. We have not established whether the two sampled failures have the same cause.
  8. Tomas: Exactly. containment can be in place while cause remains open. Controlling the material does not require us to pretend the investigation is complete.
  9. Grace: Could I ask quality about rework tonight? I need to offer production a next step without accidentally authorizing work myself.
  10. Tomas: Request a disposition review. Do not tell anyone to rework or use material before the required decision, instructions, and evaluation are in place.
  11. Grace: Then tonight is a request for review, not an expected release. I should give production the actual status and the next update we can commit to.
  12. Tomas: Keep release status separate from the deadline. Schedule pressure can make the review urgent; it cannot provide evidence or approval.
  13. Grace: The supplier offers to replace the two failed samples. That might address those particular pieces, but does not explain the dimensional problem.
  14. Tomas: Replacement could be a correction through the approved process. It would not by itself establish the cause or prevent the same issue recurring.
  15. Grace: I will request their manufacturing and inspection records. We should also examine our measurement setup rather than begin by assigning fault to the supplier.
  16. Tomas: Include our instrument's calibration status and the relevant results. A certificate or a suspicion is not a substitute for the evidence from either side.

  19. Grace: For now: three hundred held, two sampled failures, cause open, no release or rework authorized. I will send that exact status to production.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two nonconforming lots still await disposition

Fictional incoming-material review: two lots are identified as nonconforming; investigation is open and disposition is unauthorized.

  1. Production planner: Two lots were inspected. Can I show the incoming-material issue as resolved on the schedule?
  2. Supplier quality engineer: No. Both lots are identified as nonconforming, and the investigation and disposition remain open.
  3. Production planner: The inspection is finished, though. What completed work can I accurately report?
  4. Supplier quality engineer: The nonconformance was identified in two lots. That doesn't establish authorization to use or release them.
  5. Production planner: Should I assume the supplier CAPA is complete because we've sent them the findings?
  6. Supplier quality engineer: No. Sending findings is a communication step, not evidence of completed corrective action.
  7. Production planner: I'll keep material availability unconfirmed and ask for the next investigation update.
  8. Supplier quality engineer: Please preserve the two lot references so the planning discussion maintains lot traceability.
  9. Production planner: I'll link the records and avoid substituting another lot without a separate verified decision.
  10. Supplier quality engineer: I'll communicate any authorized status change and distinguish it from our ongoing assessment activities.
A supplier response is not an accepted CAPA

Separate fictional quality case: a supplier submitted a corrective-action response for lot D12, but the receiving quality team has not reviewed it.

  1. Procurement lead: The supplier sent its CAPA response for D12. Can I mark the issue closed?
  2. Quality reviewer: Receipt is complete, but our review and any acceptance decision are still pending.
  3. Procurement lead: They wrote resolved in the response. Doesn't that settle the status for our dashboard?
  4. Quality reviewer: That is the supplier's statement, not a completed assessment by the responsible receiving team.
  5. Procurement lead: What distinction should I communicate to production while the review continues?
  6. Quality reviewer: Supplier response received, assessment pending, and no new material-use authorization established by this exchange.
  7. Procurement lead: I'll retain the response against the nonconformance record rather than replace our status with their wording.
  8. Quality reviewer: Good. Keep lot traceability so the response isn't accidentally applied to unrelated material.
  9. Procurement lead: Can you provide a review-status checkpoint once an owner has confirmed the work?
  10. Quality reviewer: Yes. I'll report the verified next step without treating a supplier declaration as proof of effectiveness or closure.
Process trials complete, validation review pending

Separate fictional manufacturing update: three planned process trials have been executed, but the process-validation report has not been reviewed.

  1. Manufacturing manager: All three trials ran. Can the status slide say the process is validated?
  2. Validation engineer: Execution is complete, but the process-validation report still awaits review.
  3. Manufacturing manager: Would changing the phrase to fully tested avoid that approval issue?
  4. Validation engineer: It could still overstate the scope. Say the three planned trials were executed, with review pending.
  5. Manufacturing manager: Do we have a confirmed conclusion about acceptance criteria from the information supplied here?
  6. Validation engineer: No. We should not invent a pass, a failure, or an authorized manufacturing decision.
  7. Manufacturing manager: I'll distinguish completed trial activity from the unconfirmed assessment and its production implications.
  8. Validation engineer: Please keep the material and lot references available so the trial evidence remains traceable.
  9. Manufacturing manager: I'll ask for the next review checkpoint rather than promise routine production availability.
  10. Validation engineer: I'll provide that status when confirmed and identify any remaining evidence needs without announcing an outcome prematurely.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three hundred pieces on hold
  1. Read the supplier exchange. State 120 plus 180 equals 300 held, with two observed sampled failures.
  2. Swap roles. Keep both lots held, cause open, and release or rework unapproved despite the certificate and deadline.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Held does not mean every piece failed

A lot contains 90 pieces. Two sampled pieces failed a check. The whole lot is held, and no rework or release is authorized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Planner: "The held quantity is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. ninety

The hold covers the whole ninety-piece lot, not just the inspected failures.

2. Engineer: "The stated number of failed sampled pieces is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. two

Only two sampled failures are supplied in the case.

3. Planner: "The handling decision still needs authorized ___."

Answer and explanation

D. disposition

No decision permitting release or rework has been made.

4. Engineer: "A production deadline does not change the release ___."

Answer and explanation

A. status

Schedule urgency does not supply the missing authorization for material use.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Manufacturing, Suppliers, and Nonconformance
Original fictional case: Quality engineer Tomas and supply planner Grace review two incoming component lots containing 120 and 180 pieces. One sampled piece from each lot failed a specified dimensional check. Both lots are on hold under the fictional site procedure. The cause is not established, and no release or rework disposition is authorized. Production needs parts tomorrow. Grace proposes describing all 300 pieces as defective, then releasing the second lot because its supplier certificate says conforming.
Language workshop: Three hundred pieces on hold
Communication goal: Separate affected-lot scope, observed failures, containment, cause, and authorized disposition in a supplier discussion.
Language guidance: The held population is 300 pieces. The supplied observation is two failed sampled pieces, one from each lot. Do not call all 300 individually confirmed defective, or treat uninspected pieces as conforming. The hold and the observed failures describe different scopes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both lots are on hold, totaling 300 pieces. / One sampled piece from each lot failed the dimensional check. / We have not inspected and confirmed every piece as defective. / The uninspected pieces are not thereby proven conforming. / Reference the specified dimension and applicable tolerance. / No release or rework disposition is authorized. / I understand tomorrow's need; the material status still has to be respected. / The certificate does not erase the failed inspection result. / The hold limits unintended use while the issue is assessed. / The supplier cause has not been established. / Ask for evidence addressing the specified nonconformance. / Fixing a piece and preventing recurrence are different objectives. / Include calibration status and relevant measurement information in the review. / Use lot records to assess what other material may be affected. / Only the designated process can approve the material disposition. / Give production the confirmed status and the next review time, not an assumed release.
Vocabulary: incoming inspection: Examination of received material against specified requirements. / nonconformance: Failure to fulfill a specified requirement. / nonconforming material: Material identified as not meeting a specified requirement. / containment: Immediate action to limit the scope or spread of a problem. / quarantine: Controlled segregation or status preventing unintended use. / disposition: An authorized decision on how identified material will be handled. / rework: Action intended to bring nonconforming product into conformity. / scrap: Material designated not to be used for its intended production purpose. / concession: An authorized permission to use or release specified nonconforming product under defined conditions. / certificate of conformity: A supplier statement that supplied material meets specified requirements. / supplier corrective action request: A formal request for a supplier to address a quality problem. / corrective action: Action addressing the cause of a nonconformity to prevent recurrence. / correction: Action addressing a detected nonconformity itself. / CAPA: Corrective and preventive action within a quality-system process. / lot genealogy: Records linking material lots through production and distribution. / acceptance sampling: Evaluation of a lot using a defined sample and decision plan. / dimensional tolerance: The permitted variation in a specified measurement. / measurement uncertainty: The quantified uncertainty associated with a measurement result. / calibration status: Whether a measuring instrument's calibration requirements are satisfied. / supplier qualification: Evaluation and approval of a supplier for a defined purpose. / change notification: Communication of a proposed or implemented change affecting supply. / release status: The current authorization state for using or distributing material. / Quality Management System Regulation: The current US device quality-system regulation, commonly called QMSR. / ISO 13485: An international standard for medical-device quality management systems.
Speaking task: Read the supplier exchange. State 120 plus 180 equals 300 held, with two observed sampled failures. Swap roles. Keep both lots held, cause open, and release or rework unapproved despite the certificate and deadline. Read the corrected ninety-piece transfer. Distinguish ninety held from two sampled failures and a disposition still required.
Partner: Tomas: Quality engineer / Grace: Supply planner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A lot contains 90 pieces. Two sampled pieces failed a check. The whole lot is held, and no rework or release is authorized.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Grace: Once a cause is supported, I will ask what action addresses it and how effectiveness will be checked. A promise about future lots is not enough.
Tomas: That is the purpose of corrective action. Distinguish the action against recurrence from replacing a piece or maintaining the current hold.
Grace: For now: three hundred held, two sampled failures, cause open, no release or rework authorized. I will send that exact status to production.
Tomas: Use lot genealogy to check whether other material may be involved. Keep any wider scope qualified until the records support it.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Original fictional case: Quality engineer Tomas and supply planner Grace review two incoming component lots containing 120 and 180 pieces. One sampled piece from each lot failed a specified dimensional check. Both lots are on hold under the fictional site procedure. The cause is not established, and no release or rework disposition is authorized. Production needs parts tomorrow. Grace proposes describing all 300 pieces as defective, then releasing the second lot because its supplier certificate says conforming.
Language workshop: Three hundred pieces on hold
Communication goal: Separate affected-lot scope, observed failures, containment, cause, and authorized disposition in a supplier discussion.
Language guidance: The held population is 300 pieces. The supplied observation is two failed sampled pieces, one from each lot. Do not call all 300 individually confirmed defective, or treat uninspected pieces as conforming. The hold and the observed failures describe different scopes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both lots are on hold, totaling 300 pieces. / One sampled piece from each lot failed the dimensional check. / We have not inspected and confirmed every piece as defective. / The uninspected pieces are not thereby proven conforming. / Reference the specified dimension and applicable tolerance. / No release or rework disposition is authorized. / I understand tomorrow's need; the material status still has to be respected. / The certificate does not erase the failed inspection result. / The hold limits unintended use while the issue is assessed. / The supplier cause has not been established. / Ask for evidence addressing the specified nonconformance. / Fixing a piece and preventing recurrence are different objectives. / Include calibration status and relevant measurement information in the review. / Use lot records to assess what other material may be affected. / Only the designated process can approve the material disposition. / Give production the confirmed status and the next review time, not an assumed release.
Vocabulary: incoming inspection: Examination of received material against specified requirements. / nonconformance: Failure to fulfill a specified requirement. / nonconforming material: Material identified as not meeting a specified requirement. / containment: Immediate action to limit the scope or spread of a problem. / quarantine: Controlled segregation or status preventing unintended use. / disposition: An authorized decision on how identified material will be handled. / rework: Action intended to bring nonconforming product into conformity. / scrap: Material designated not to be used for its intended production purpose. / concession: An authorized permission to use or release specified nonconforming product under defined conditions. / certificate of conformity: A supplier statement that supplied material meets specified requirements. / supplier corrective action request: A formal request for a supplier to address a quality problem. / corrective action: Action addressing the cause of a nonconformity to prevent recurrence. / correction: Action addressing a detected nonconformity itself. / CAPA: Corrective and preventive action within a quality-system process. / lot genealogy: Records linking material lots through production and distribution. / acceptance sampling: Evaluation of a lot using a defined sample and decision plan. / dimensional tolerance: The permitted variation in a specified measurement. / measurement uncertainty: The quantified uncertainty associated with a measurement result. / calibration status: Whether a measuring instrument's calibration requirements are satisfied. / supplier qualification: Evaluation and approval of a supplier for a defined purpose. / change notification: Communication of a proposed or implemented change affecting supply. / release status: The current authorization state for using or distributing material. / Quality Management System Regulation: The current US device quality-system regulation, commonly called QMSR. / ISO 13485: An international standard for medical-device quality management systems.
Speaking task: Read the supplier exchange. State 120 plus 180 equals 300 held, with two observed sampled failures. Swap roles. Keep both lots held, cause open, and release or rework unapproved despite the certificate and deadline. Read the corrected ninety-piece transfer. Distinguish ninety held from two sampled failures and a disposition still required.
Partner: Tomas: Quality engineer / Grace: Supply planner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A lot contains 90 pieces. Two sampled pieces failed a check. The whole lot is held, and no rework or release is authorized.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Grace: Once a cause is supported, I will ask what action addresses it and how effectiveness will be checked. A promise about future lots is not enough.
Tomas: That is the purpose of corrective action. Distinguish the action against recurrence from replacing a piece or maintaining the current hold.
Grace: For now: three hundred held, two sampled failures, cause open, no release or rework authorized. I will send that exact status to production.
Tomas: Use lot genealogy to check whether other material may be involved. Keep any wider scope qualified until the records support it.
END REFERENCE
08Clinical Training and Labeling BoundariesA useful question outside this session+

Your goal

Acknowledge an out-of-scope product-use request, keep the demonstration within authorized materials, and arrange a precise follow-up.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

A useful question outside this session

Clinical trainer Sora is delivering a fictional device session using version 4 of internally approved materials for adult outpatient use. Clinician Alex asks for a pediatric home-use demonstration that the session materials do not cover. A version 3 slide remains in Sora's folder, but its current status has not been checked. No expanded-use authorization or reviewed response is supplied. Sora must acknowledge the question, avoid improvising the requested demonstration, and route it without implying that the clinician's question is improper.

Sora: Clinical trainer / Alex: Clinician attending the session

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What do the current session materials cover?

Answer and explanation

C. Adult outpatient use, version 4

The brief limits the internally approved session materials to adult outpatient use in version four.

2. What is missing for the requested demonstration?

Answer and explanation

C. Reviewed materials covering the requested pediatric home-use context

Neither the request nor an unchecked old slide supplies the reviewed scope for the demonstration.

3. What should Sora avoid implying?

Answer and explanation

B. That the question is improper merely because it is outside the session scope

The scenario calls for a respectful boundary, not a judgment that asking the question is itself improper.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Internally approved training material is not the same thing as a new regulatory authorization. A version number in a folder does not establish that the document is current. Confirm status through the controlled source rather than using an unchecked older slide.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
labeling
Device-related written, printed, or graphic information accompanying or relating to the product.review current labeling
instructions for use
The device's documented directions and relevant use information.consult the instructions for use
indication
A specified condition or purpose for which use is described.confirm the stated indication
contraindication
A circumstance in which the device should not be used.identify a contraindication
warning
Information highlighting a significant hazard or safety concern.communicate the relevant warning
precaution
Information about care needed to avoid problems during appropriate use.explain a precaution
training scope
The users, tasks, settings, and content covered by a session.define the training scope
approved material
Content authorized through the relevant internal review process.use current approved materials
version control
Management of revisions so the appropriate version is used.maintain version control
obsolete copy
A superseded document that is no longer the current controlled version.remove an obsolete copy
demonstration unit
A device or model designated for showing functions under specified conditions.identify the demonstration unit
simulation
A representation of a task or setting used for training or evaluation.conduct a training simulation
in-service training
Instruction delivered to professionals in their work context.schedule in-service training
competency assessment
Evaluation against defined knowledge or performance criteria.distinguish attendance from competency assessment
attendance record
A record that a person participated in a session.maintain an attendance record
scope boundary
The limit of what a session or role is authorized to cover.state the scope boundary
off-label use
Use outside the product's authorized labeling, where that concept applies.route an off-label-use question
unsolicited question
A question initiated by the recipient rather than prompted by the presenter.document an unsolicited question
medical information request
A request routed for an appropriate reviewed scientific or product response.route a medical information request
promotional claim
A statement used to encourage product selection or use.review a promotional claim
disclaimer
A statement explaining a limitation or qualification.avoid relying on a disclaimer alone
escalation route
The designated path for a question beyond the current role or scope.follow the escalation route
follow-up commitment
A specified promise about the next communication or action.make a realistic follow-up commitment
teach-back
Asking a learner to restate or demonstrate key information to check understanding.use teach-back appropriately

Outside this session is narrower than clinically forbidden. The trainer should not turn a session boundary into unsupported medical or legal advice. A disclaimer does not make an otherwise unsupported demonstration appropriate; route the specific question through the applicable process.

C Notice the language

A boundary can include a next step

A useful response names what you can cover now and what needs another route. Do not shame the questioner or pretend the question was answered by a disclaimer. Keep product training, a clinician's independent judgment, and the organization's approved response process distinct.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the question
That is a specific and useful question about a different population and setting.
State the session scope
Today we are covering adult outpatient use with version 4 materials.
Name the boundary
These materials do not cover pediatric home use.
Decline the demonstration
I cannot improvise that demonstration within this session.
Avoid judging the question
The limitation is the scope of my materials, not your ability to ask.
Offer the next route
I can document the question for the appropriate reviewed response.
Keep the request precise
I will preserve the population and setting you asked about.
Check the source
I need to confirm the current controlled version before using that slide.
Separate approval types
Internal material approval does not create an expanded product authorization.
Avoid a disclaimer shortcut
Adding a disclaimer would not resolve the missing reviewed content.
Respect clinical roles
I am not making a patient-specific treatment recommendation.
Clarify records
Session attendance is not the same as a completed competency assessment.
Bound the promise
I can confirm that the request was routed; I cannot promise a particular answer.
Keep the demonstration aligned
We will continue with the functions covered by the current session materials.
Check understanding
Please restate which use context today's session covers.
Close respectfully
I will follow up through the designated route while we keep this session within scope.
6 language and precision notes
Approved
Identify whether the approval concerns materials, product marketing, or another decision.
Current
Means the valid controlled version, not merely the latest file someone remembers.
Outside scope
States a boundary without making a universal clinical prohibition.
Cannot demonstrate
A role-and-material limit, not a claim that the question is illegitimate.
Will route
Promises an action, not the outcome of the reviewed response.
Attended
Confirms participation; it does not automatically establish competence.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which reply is best supported?

Answer and explanation

D. These materials cover adult outpatient use; I can route your pediatric home-use question for a reviewed response.

The reply respects the question while accurately limiting the current materials and offering a review route.

2. How should Sora handle the version 3 slide?

Answer and explanation

D. Check its status against the controlled source before using it.

The folder copy has unverified status and cannot establish that the content is current.

3. The attendance sheet is complete after the session. What does that establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Recorded participation in this session, not performance against competency criteria

Attendance records presence. Competence needs the defined assessment, while expanded-use authorization is a separate question that a signature or discussion cannot establish.

4. Which follow-up commitment is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

C. I will route the exact question and confirm the next communication through the designated process.

The trainer can commit to routing and communication without promising an unsupported substantive outcome.

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sora: Clinical trainer / Alex: Clinician attending the session

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
